You're right — I was thinking too small. The real flow is:

1. **Define a project** → the LLM decomposes it into tasks (with test suites)
2. **Feed tasks to the swarm** → agents solve them via the LangGraph loop
3. **Trajectories train LoRA adapters** (the hypernetwork parametric memory)
4. **Subsequent tasks load those adapters** → the model improves as it goes

```
     1→# Rune
     2→
     3→**Treating LoRA weight space as episodic memory for unbounded context in local coding agents**
     4→
     5→---
     6→
     7→## Vision
     8→
     9→### The Context Window Problem
    10→
    11→Current coding agents face a fundamental architectural constraint: as codebases grow, token-based context windows become bloated, expensive, and ultimately insufficient. Even with 400K-token windows, maintaining state across long coding sessions requires quadratic compute overhead and suffers from "needle in a haystack" retrieval failures. For local Small Language Models (SLMs) under 10B parameters, this limitation is existential.
    12→
    13→Rune addresses this by **moving state representation from the token-level context window into the model's weight-space** via dynamic, task-specific LoRA adapters. Rather than stuffing ever-more tokens into context, we encode procedural knowledge—architecture specifications, execution feedback, coding conventions—directly into the model's parameters as modular, composable memory units.
    14→
    15→### The Solution: Parametric Episodic Memory
    16→
    17→Rune implements a **recursive code generation loop** where each iteration:
    18→
    19→1. **Generates code** using the current adapter state
    20→2. **Executes** in a sandboxed environment
    21→3. **Distills feedback** (errors, test results, architectural constraints) into LoRA weight updates via a hypernetwork
    22→4. **Composes memory** by merging task-specific adapters into a persistent library
    23→
    24→**Initial Bootstrapping**: Before the first code generation iteration, the hypernetwork processes the project specification—goals, implementation rules, architecture conventions, and organizational structure—to create an initial project-specific adapter. This ensures the base model is immediately contextualized to the project's requirements, rather than starting from a generic coding baseline.
    25→
```

```
     1→This is an excellent paper with highly relevant insights for Rune. The **Programming by Backprop (PBB)** research directly validates and extends our core thesis about parametric episodic memory. Here are the key insights we should incorporate: [arxiv](https://www.arxiv.org/abs/2506.18777)
     2→
     3→## Critical Findings from Programming by Backprop
     4→
     5→**1. Code Internalizes Algorithmic Abstractions via Backpropagation** [arxiv](https://www.arxiv.org/abs/2506.18777)
     6→
     7→The paper demonstrates that LLMs trained on source code *without* input-output examples can still evaluate those programs on new inputs. This happens through standard backpropagation—the model learns to encode procedural logic directly in its weights. This is precisely what Rune does with LoRA adapters.
     8→
     9→**2. Code > Natural Language for Abstraction Learning** [arxiv](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.18777.pdf)
    10→
    11→PBB works "significantly better when programs are provided as code rather than semantically equivalent language descriptions." This validates our approach of encoding project specifications, architecture rules, and execution feedback as *structured code representations* rather than prose documentation.
    12→
    13→**3. Compositional Evaluation from Independent Training** [themoonlight](https://www.themoonlight.io/en/review/programming-by-backprop-llms-acquire-reusable-algorithmic-abstractions-during-code-training)
    14→
    15→Models can evaluate *compositions* of programs they were trained on independently. This directly supports our Evolution Operator's consolidation strategy—merging adapters trained on separate trajectories should enable compositional reasoning about combined functionality.
    16→
    17→**4. Robustness Across Input Distributions** [themoonlight](https://www.themoonlight.io/en/review/programming-by-backprop-llms-acquire-reusable-algorithmic-abstractions-during-code-training)
    18→
    19→Code-trained models via PBB show "more robust evaluation across input variations" compared to I/O-trained models. This mitigates the "embers of autoregression" problem where models overfit to demonstration distributions. Our adapter-based memory should exhibit similar robustness.
    20→
    21→**5. Chain-of-Thought Enhances Reliability** [arxiv](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.18777.pdf)
    22→
    23→While models can implicitly evaluate programs in the forward pass, explicit chain-of-thought "stepping through the program" is more reliable, especially for longer programs. This informs our System-2 reasoning loop design.
    24→
    25→## Specific Modifications for Rune
    26→
    27→I should update the documentation to incorporate these insights:
    28→
    29→### 1. **Training Data Strategy** (Phase 1.4)
    30→- Include successful execution trajectories *without* input-output pairs in hypernetwork training
    31→- Train on code representations of architectural patterns, not just prose documentation
    32→- This enables PBB-style internalization of procedural abstractions
    33→
    34→### 2. **Hypernetwork Training Objective** (Phase 4.2)
    35→- Add a PBB-inspired objective: adapter should enable program evaluation without explicit I/O context
    36→- Test: generate adapter from code, evaluate on held-out inputs without those inputs in context window
    37→
    38→### 3. **Chain-of-Thought Integration** (Phase 2.3)
    39→- System-2 reasoning should include explicit step-through execution traces
    40→- These traces become training signal for hypernetwork
    41→- Validates Cook et al.'s finding that CoT improves reliability
    42→
    43→### 4. **Compositional Memory Validation** (Phase 3.2)
    44→- Test that merged adapters enable evaluation of composite programs
    45→- Example: merge "authentication" + "database access" adapters → can execute combined auth+DB logic
    46→- Direct validation of PBB's compositional generalization findings
    47→
    48→### 5. **Code-First Encoding** (Throughout)
    49→- Emphasize that project specifications, architectural constraints, and conventions should be encoded as *executable code examples* or *code-like structured representations*
    50→- Not prose documentation—actual code that demonstrates the abstraction
    51→
    52→
```

```
     1→## Abstract
     2→
     3→<div class="abstract" markdown="1">
     4→**Abstract.** Local coding agents such as Aider, Cursor, and Claude Code operate within fixed context windows and lose procedural knowledge between sessions. Architectural decisions, recurring bug patterns, and project-specific idioms are rediscovered from scratch each time. No mechanism exists to accumulate session-derived knowledge as persistent, composable model parameters.
     5→
     6→Rune proposes parametric episodic memory for local coding agents: each coding session produces a write-once LoRA adapter encoding procedural knowledge as parameter deltas in the base model's weight space. Adapters are composable, non-destructive, and reversible, enabling selective retrieval without overwriting previously acquired knowledge. This places Rune in the composable weight-space category of memory strategies, distinct from RAG (token-space) and full fine-tuning (destructive weight-space).
     7→
     8→The **proposed** mechanism extends Doc-to-LoRA -- a hypernetwork architecture **validated** on document question-answering -- to code execution trajectories: sequences of generation attempts, execution results, and reflection steps. The hypernetwork maps trajectory inputs to LoRA adapter weights in a single forward pass. This trajectory modality extension has not been empirically validated; Doc-to-LoRA's applicability to code trajectories is the central open research question. An Evolution Operator governs the adapter lifecycle using a fitness function combining pass rate and generalization score, performing consolidate, update, forget, and merge operations.
     9→
    10→If the Phase 1 kill-switch gate succeeds, Rune is **expected** to deliver parametric episodic memory for coding agents, addressing the missing capability identified by Pink et al. The fitness-driven adapter lifecycle is **specified** in the architecture and will be evaluated empirically against LoRA Soups' composition-by-assumption approach. The trajectory modality extension and the PBB-inspired procedural behavior criterion are **proposed** and require empirical validation.
    11→</div>
    12→
```

     1→## Methods
     2→
     3→Building on the theoretical foundations established in the Background section — LoRA decomposition, hypernetwork architectures, PBB's procedural encoding findings, and the trajectory modality argument — this section specifies Rune's concrete architecture, algorithms, and design decisions. Each subsection identifies the components, their interactions, and the claim tier for every non-trivial design choice. Where Background introduced a concept, Methods references it without re-derivation. Where Methods introduces new formalism, it is marked as such.
     4→
     5→---
     6→
     7→### System Architecture Overview
     8→
     9→Rune is structured as five services collaborating around a central adapter registry. Each service owns a single responsibility; inter-service communication occurs via HTTP APIs and a shared filesystem-backed adapter store.
    10→
    11→**rune-agent** is the core execution service. It implements a LangGraph `StateGraph` with four nodes — `generate`, `execute`, `reflect`, and `save_trajectory` — connected by conditional routing that terminates on test success or attempt exhaustion. The agent receives a task description and test suite, selects adapters from the registry based on task metadata, and runs the recursive generate-execute-reflect loop until a solution passes or attempts are exhausted. The complete trajectory is then persisted for downstream distillation. The loop structure is **specified** — it is implemented in the codebase and tested.
    12→
    13→**lora-server** wraps vLLM with optional pipeline parallelism and dynamic adapter loading following the S-LoRA unified paging pattern.[^sheng2023slora] It serves the base model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) with QLoRA quantization and exposes an OpenAI-compatible API that accepts adapter selection metadata per request. The serving configuration is discussed in detail in the Training Data Strategy and Serving Architecture subsection.
    14→
    15→**training-svc** manages adapter production. In Phase 3, it runs direct LoRA fine-tuning on trajectory data using PEFT utilities. In Phase 4, it hosts the hypernetwork that replaces gradient descent with a single forward pass. Training jobs acquire a GPU lease from lora-server, which yields one GPU for the duration of the training job.
    16→
    17→**evolution-svc** manages the adapter lifecycle — consolidation, update, archival, and experimental merging — using fitness-driven selection rather than naive composition. Its operations are detailed in the Evolution Operator subsection.
    18→
    19→**adapter-registry** is the shared dependency root: a SQLite-backed metadata store paired with safetensors adapter files on the local filesystem. Every service reads from or writes to the registry. Adapters are write-once and immutable after creation; metadata fields (fitness score, archive status) are mutable. The three-level hierarchy (project, domain, task) is encoded in both the filesystem path convention and the SQLite schema.
    20→
    21→The agent loop is implemented as a LangGraph `StateGraph` with four nodes — `generate`, `execute`, `reflect`, and `save_trajectory` — connected by conditional routing that terminates on test success or attempt exhaustion.
    22→
    23→**Claim tiers:** The five-service architecture and loop structure are **specified** — they are implemented in the codebase. Phase 0 and Phase 1 empirical performance on coding tasks is **TBD**.
    24→
    25→---
    26→
    27→### Doc-to-LoRA Hypernetwork Adaptation
    28→
    29→The Background section established that Doc-to-LoRA validates the hypernetwork-to-LoRA mechanism: a hypernetwork takes a document as input and produces LoRA adapter weights in a single forward pass, with no gradient-based fine-tuning at inference time.[^charakorn2026doc2lora] The generated adapter encodes the document's content into the base model's parameter space via the low-rank decomposition \(\Delta W = BA\) established in Background.[^hu2021lora]
    30→
    31→Doc-to-LoRA was validated on document QA tasks — retrieving specific facts from long textual inputs. It was not validated on coding trajectories, procedural knowledge, or code execution traces. Rune proposes extending the hypernetwork mechanism to a structurally different input modality: code execution trajectories containing sequential (code, execution result, reflection) triples. This extension is **proposed** and requires Phase 1 empirical validation.
    32→
    33→Rune's hypernetwork defines the following formal mapping (new equation):
    34→
    35→\[
    36→H: \mathcal{T} \rightarrow (B, A), \quad B \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times r}, \; A \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times k}
    37→\]
    38→
    39→where \(\mathcal{T}\) denotes the trajectory space — a sequence of (code, execution result, reflection) triples produced by the recursive loop. The hypernetwork \(H\) takes a complete trajectory and produces the LoRA weight matrices \(B\) and \(A\) that, when applied to the base model via the \(\Delta W = BA\) decomposition from Background, encode the procedural knowledge from that trajectory into the model's parameter space. The hypernetwork produces per-layer \((B, A)\) pairs — one for each targeted transformer layer — though the equation above shows a single pair for notational clarity.
    40→
    41→The architecture of \(H\) consists of two stages. First, a variable-length trajectory encoder processes the trajectory into a fixed-size latent representation. The encoder must handle trajectories of varying length (different numbers of attempts, different code lengths per attempt). The proposed encoder design uses a Perceiver-based cross-attention pooling mechanism:[^ha2016hypernetworks] a set of learned latent queries attend to the trajectory token sequence, producing a fixed-dimensional representation regardless of input length. This Perceiver-based encoder is a **proposed architecture choice and Phase 1 ablation target** — alternative encoders (mean pooling, hierarchical attention, recurrent summarization) will be compared during Phase 1 hypernetwork development.
    42→
    43→Second, per-layer output heads map the fixed-size latent representation to \((B, A)\) matrices for each targeted transformer layer. Each output head is a linear projection from the latent dimension to the flattened \(B\) and \(A\) matrices for one layer.
    44→
    45→For trajectories exceeding the encoder's context limit, the trajectory is chunked at attempt boundaries (each attempt is one chunk), and chunk representations are averaged before being passed to the output heads. This chunking mechanism preserves the self-contained structure of individual attempts while allowing arbitrarily long trajectories to be processed.
    46→
    47→The three structural properties of code execution trajectories identified in Background — temporal ordering, procedural abstraction, and self-correcting structure — motivate the choice of trajectories as input modality. These properties distinguish trajectories from documents (unordered factual content) and from few-shot examples (static input-output pairs), and are the basis for expecting the hypernetwork mechanism to transfer from documents to trajectories.
    48→
    49→**Claim tiers:** The hypernetwork-to-LoRA mechanism is **expected** to extend from documents to trajectories based on the structural analogy established in Background. Trajectory-to-adapter quality on coding tasks is **proposed** — it is the central empirical hypothesis requiring Phase 1 validation. The Perceiver-based encoder architecture is an **ablation target** — it will be compared against alternatives during Phase 1 development.
    50→
    51→---
    52→
    53→### Adapter Distillation Pipeline
    54→
    55→The distillation pipeline has two implementation paths depending on the development phase. Phase 3 uses direct LoRA fine-tuning as a bootstrapping path — a validated technique that establishes the training infrastructure and produces the first adapter corpus. Phase 4 replaces gradient descent with the hypernetwork forward pass described above — the target architecture that eliminates per-adapter training cost at inference time. The pseudocode below covers both paths.
    56→
    57→```text
    58→Algorithm: Rune Recursive Loop
    59→-------------------------------------------------------------
    60→Input:
    61→  task_description : str            (RuneState.task_description)
    62→  test_suite       : str            (RuneState.test_suite)
    63→  adapter_ids      : list[str]      (RuneState.adapter_ids)
    64→  max_attempts     : int            (RuneState.max_attempts)
    65→
    66→Output:
    67→  trajectory       : list[AttemptRecord]  (RuneState.trajectory)
    68→  outcome          : 'success' | 'exhausted'  (RuneState.outcome)
    69→
    70→Procedure:
    71→  attempt_count <- 0
    72→  trajectory <- []
    73→
    74→  loop:
    75→    -- generate node --------------------------------------------------
    76→    generated_code <- generate(task_description, trajectory, adapter_ids)
    77→
    78→    -- execute node ----------------------------------------------------
    79→    stdout, stderr, exit_code, tests_passed <- execute(generated_code, test_suite)
    80→
    81→    -- reflect node ----------------------------------------------------
    82→    record <- AttemptRecord(attempt_count + 1, generated_code,
    83→                           stdout, stderr, exit_code, tests_passed)
    84→    trajectory.append(record)
    85→    attempt_count <- attempt_count + 1
    86→
    87→    -- should_retry routing --------------------------------------------
    88→    if tests_passed OR attempt_count >= max_attempts:
    89→      break  -> save_trajectory
    90→    else:
    91→      continue  -> generate
    92→
    93→  -- save_trajectory node ----------------------------------------------
    94→  outcome <- 'success' if tests_passed else 'exhausted'
    95→
    96→  -- distillation path (phase-dependent) -------------------------------
    97→  if Phase == 3:  // bootstrapping implementation (specified)
    98→    adapter <- direct_lora_finetune(trajectory)
    99→  elif Phase == 4:  // target implementation (proposed)
   100→    adapter <- H(trajectory)  // hypernetwork forward pass
   101→
   102→  adapter_registry.store(adapter, outcome, trajectory)
   103→  return trajectory, outcome
   104→-------------------------------------------------------------
   105→```
   106→
   107→Each node in the pseudocode maps directly to the LangGraph `StateGraph` implementation:
   108→
   109→**`generate`** receives the task description, the trajectory accumulated so far (empty on the first attempt), and the selected adapter IDs. It calls the base model (served by lora-server with the specified adapters loaded) to produce a candidate code solution. On subsequent attempts, the full trajectory — prior code, execution outputs, error messages — is included in the prompt, providing the model with debugging context. The `generated_code` field in `RuneState` stores the output.
   110→
   111→**`execute`** runs the generated code against the test suite in an isolated Docker sandbox. The sandbox is network-isolated, memory-limited, and CPU-limited. Execution produces four fields in `RuneState`: `stdout`, `stderr`, `exit_code`, and `tests_passed`.
   112→
   113→**`reflect`** appends the current attempt's data to the trajectory and increments `attempt_count`. Each `AttemptRecord` in the `RuneState.trajectory` list contains the attempt number, generated code, execution output, and pass/fail status.
   114→
   115→**`should_retry`** is the conditional routing function: if `tests_passed` is true, the loop terminates via `save_trajectory` (success). If `attempt_count >= max_attempts`, the loop terminates via `save_trajectory` (exhausted). Otherwise, the loop returns to `generate` for another attempt.
   116→
   117→**`save_trajectory`** records the terminal `outcome` ('success' or 'exhausted') and persists the complete trajectory to the adapter registry for downstream distillation.
   118→
   119→The Phase 3 distillation path (`direct_lora_finetune`) is **specified** — it uses standard PEFT fine-tuning on trajectory data, a validated technique that will be implemented first to establish the training pipeline and produce the initial adapter corpus. The Phase 4 distillation path (`H(trajectory)`) is **proposed** — it replaces per-adapter gradient descent with the hypernetwork forward pass, eliminating training cost at inference time. The Phase 4 path depends on successful hypernetwork training in Phase 1.
   120→
   121→---
   122→
   123→### Evolution Operator
   124→
   125→The Background section established that LoRA Soups' CAT method can outperform data mixing for binary skill composition tasks,[^prabhakar2024lorasoups] but also that orthogonality between merged LoRA modules does not guarantee semantic disentanglement[^zhang2025orthogonality] and that adapter merging can reactivate latent reasoning traces.[^zou2026merging] The Evolution Operator is Rune's approach to fitness-driven adapter lifecycle management — testing adapter quality empirically before promotion rather than assuming composition is safe by default.
   126→
   127→The Evolution Operator defines four operations on the adapter registry:
   128→
   129→1. **Consolidate** (**specified**): Merge related adapters across task types into a generalized adapter when similarity exceeds a configurable threshold. The output is a new registry entry at a higher hierarchy level (e.g., task-level adapters consolidated into a domain-level adapter). The input adapters are archived — not deleted, per the write-once policy — and remain queryable but ineligible for retrieval routing.
   130→
   131→2. **Update** (**specified**): When a new trajectory produces an adapter with higher fitness than an existing adapter of the same task type, the old adapter is archived and the new adapter is registered. This enforces the write-once, archive-not-delete policy: no adapter is overwritten, but the registry always routes to the highest-fitness entry for each task type.
   132→
   133→3. **Forget** (**specified**): The `archive_adapter()` function sets `is_archived = 1` in the SQLite registry. Archived adapters are never deleted from disk — they become ineligible for retrieval routing but remain available for historical analysis, lineage tracking, and potential reactivation.
   134→
   135→4. **Merge** (**ablation target**): Combine adapter weight matrices from two adapters into a single composite entry. This operation is labeled as an **ablation target** due to the orthogonality and latent trace concerns identified by Zhang et al.[^zhang2025orthogonality] and Zou.[^zou2026merging] Merge will be tested empirically — if composed adapters produce degraded or incoherent behavior, the operation will be disabled in favor of single-adapter selection.
   136→
   137→The Evolution Operator uses a fitness criterion to drive all lifecycle decisions. The fitness function for an adapter \(a\) is defined as (new equation):
   138→
   139→\[
   140→\phi(a) = \alpha \cdot \text{pass\_rate}(a) + (1 - \alpha) \cdot \text{generalization}(a)
   141→\]
   142→
   143→where \(\text{pass\_rate}(a)\) is the adapter's test-passing rate on held-out tasks of the same type, and \(\text{generalization}(a)\) is a cross-task coverage metric measuring the adapter's performance on tasks outside its original training domain. The weighting parameter \(\alpha\) controls the trade-off between specialization (high pass rate on similar tasks) and generalization (broad utility across task types). The default value is \(\alpha = 0.7\), reflecting a preference for specialization — an adapter that reliably solves its own task type is more valuable than one that partially solves many types. The \(\alpha\) parameter is an **ablation target** (default 0.7, pending Phase 4 tuning). The exact generalization metric is **TBD** — it will be defined during Phase 4 when sufficient adapter diversity exists to measure cross-task performance. The `fitness_score` column in the SQLite `adapters` table stores the computed \(\phi(a)\) value.
   144→
   145→**Contrast with LoRA Soups:** LoRA Soups' CAT method combines adapters at the data-mixing level — concatenating adapter matrices — without testing whether the resulting combination produces better behavior on downstream tasks. The combination is assumed to be beneficial based on orthogonality properties of the adapter subspaces. The Evolution Operator takes a fundamentally different approach: it tests fitness empirically before promoting any adapter to the registry, and uses fitness-driven selection to determine which adapter combinations (if any) produce beneficial behavior. This directly addresses the orthogonality gap identified by Zhang et al.:[^zhang2025orthogonality] orthogonal subspaces do not guarantee semantic coherence, so Rune tests behavioral coherence rather than assuming it. The merge operation itself is an **ablation target** — it will only be retained if empirical evaluation shows that merged adapters achieve higher fitness than their individual components.
   146→
   147→**Claim tiers:** The four operations (consolidate, update, forget, merge) are **specified** in the architecture. The fitness formula weighting \(\alpha\) is an **ablation target**. The merge operation's semantic coherence is **TBD** pending empirical validation of the orthogonality and latent trace concerns from the literature.
   148→
   149→---
   150→
   151→### Memory Composition Strategy
   152→
   153→Rune organizes adapters into a three-level hierarchy that mirrors the granularity of coding knowledge:
   154→
   155→- **Project level**: Adapters encoding broad patterns for a specific codebase — its architectural conventions, API design patterns, testing practices, and domain-specific idioms. A project-level adapter captures knowledge that is useful across all tasks within that project.
   156→
   157→- **Domain level**: Adapters encoding patterns for a category of tasks that spans projects — API design, database query optimization, algorithm implementation, error handling patterns. A domain-level adapter captures knowledge that transfers across projects for tasks of the same type.
   158→
   159→- **Task level**: Fine-grained adapters encoding patterns for a specific task type within a specific context. A task-level adapter captures the most specific knowledge — the particular patterns discovered while solving a particular class of problem.
   160→
   161→The filesystem path convention encodes this hierarchy: `~/.rune/adapters/{project|domain|task}/...`. The SQLite `adapters` table tracks `hierarchy_level`, `domain`, and `project_id` columns, enabling queries that filter by any combination of hierarchy and metadata.
   162→
   163→At inference time, the adapter router queries the registry by task metadata (`task_type`, `domain`, `project_id`) and selects the highest-fitness adapter at each applicable hierarchy level using the \(\phi(a)\) fitness criterion defined in the Evolution Operator section.
   164→
   165→**Default behavior** (**specified**): Single-adapter selection — the most relevant adapter at the most specific applicable level, loaded alone. If a task-level adapter exists with sufficient fitness, it is used. Otherwise, the router falls back to domain level, then project level. The rationale for single-adapter default is the interference risk established in Background: orthogonality between adapter subspaces does not guarantee semantic coherence when multiple adapters are applied simultaneously.
   166→
   167→**Multi-adapter composition** (**ablation target**): Additive accumulation across hierarchy levels, where the full weight delta applied to the base model is:
   168→
   169→\[
   170→\Delta W_{\text{composite}} = \Delta W_{\text{project}} + \Delta W_{\text{domain}} + \Delta W_{\text{task}}
   171→\]
   172→
   173→This compositional mode loads one adapter from each applicable hierarchy level and applies their weight deltas additively. It is the **ablation target** for Phase 5 and beyond. Multi-adapter composition will be tested against single-adapter selection once sufficient adapter diversity exists in the registry. The default remains single-adapter selection until empirical evidence supports multi-level composition.
   174→
   175→**Claim tiers:** The three-level hierarchy is **specified** — it is implemented in the adapter registry schema and filesystem layout. Single-adapter selection as the default retrieval mode is **specified**. Compositional accumulation across hierarchy levels is **proposed** and requires empirical validation of behavioral coherence. Multi-adapter composition semantics are an **ablation target** for Phase 5+.
   176→
   177→---
   178→
   179→### Training Data Strategy and Serving Architecture
   180→
   181→#### Training Data Strategy
   182→
   183→The Background section established Cook et al.'s finding that declarative instructions — including code — can substitute for up to 100 execution examples when fine-tuning LLMs.[^cook2025pbb] This result demonstrates that the format of training signal matters: procedural, instruction-like representations are more information-dense than equivalent example sets. Rune's training data strategy is grounded in this finding: code execution trajectories are the training signal, not (input, output) pairs.
   184→
   185→Each trajectory record in the SQLite `trajectories` table contains:
   186→
   187→- `task_description`: the natural language specification of the coding task
   188→- `test_suite`: the test code used for evaluation
   189→- A sequence of attempts, each containing `generated_code`, `stdout`, `stderr`, `exit_code`, and `tests_passed`
   190→- A terminal `outcome`: 'success' (tests passed) or 'exhausted' (max attempts reached without passing)
   191→
   192→Both successful and exhausted trajectories are training candidates. Successful trajectories encode complete solution patterns — from initial approach through iterative refinement to a passing solution. Exhausted trajectories encode debugging patterns, failure recognition, and partial solutions — knowledge about what does not work and why, which is valuable for avoiding similar failure modes in future tasks.
   193→
   194→PBB found that in-context instruction execution remains more reliable than weight-based procedural knowledge for the tasks they tested. This caveat applies directly to Rune: there is a gap between what can be encoded in weights via backpropagation and what can be reliably executed from those weights at inference time. Training on trajectories is **expected** to produce useful adapter representations based on PBB's findings about procedural encoding efficiency — instructions are information-dense, and trajectories are the richest procedural representation available. However, whether trajectory-trained adapters achieve reliable weight-based execution on coding tasks specifically is **proposed** and requires Phase 1 empirical validation. The gap between encoding efficiency and reliable execution from weights is the central empirical question for Rune's training data strategy.
   195→
   196→**Claim tiers:** Training on trajectories without input-output pairs is **expected** — grounded in PBB's demonstration that procedural representations are efficient for weight-based learning. Adapter quality on coding tasks specifically is **proposed** — this is Rune-specific and unvalidated.
   197→
   198→#### Serving Architecture
   199→
   200→The intended serving configuration is vLLM with QLoRA (NF4 quantized base model with bfloat16 adapter matrices) and optionally pipeline parallelism for multi-GPU setups. This is the **intended configuration pending Phase 0 empirical validation** — not an assertion of confirmed compatibility.
   201→
   202→**Pipeline parallelism rationale for multi-GPU setups:** For GPUs connected via PCIe (most consumer setups), tensor parallelism (TP) is not recommended. TP requires all-reduce synchronization at every transformer layer. Over PCIe bandwidth (~32 GB/s bidirectional), the all-reduce at every layer becomes a significant bottleneck compared to NVLink (~112 GB/s per direction). Pipeline parallelism splits the transformer layers across GPUs and transfers activations once at each layer boundary — a single point-to-point transfer per GPU boundary, respecting the PCIe bandwidth constraint. Additionally, vLLM issue #21471 documents TP + LoRA corruption on consumer GPUs without NVLink, which is absent under pipeline parallelism.
   203→
   204→**QLoRA serves the VRAM constraint:** NF4 quantization reduces the 7B-parameter base model from approximately 14 GB (bfloat16) to approximately 4 GB, leaving headroom for adapter weights and KV cache on consumer GPUs.[^dettmers2023qlora] S-LoRA unified paging manages adapter weights alongside KV cache in a shared GPU memory pool, enabling concurrent serving of multiple adapters without per-adapter VRAM reservation.[^sheng2023slora]
   205→
   206→**GPU lease mechanism:** When using multiple GPUs, lora-server can yield a secondary GPU to training-svc during training jobs. The serving process reconfigures to the remaining GPU(s) (reduced capacity but still available), and training-svc runs on the leased GPU with full VRAM access. When the training job completes, training-svc releases the lease, and lora-server restores its full configuration. Serving pauses briefly during reconfiguration but is not fully offline during training.
   207→
   208→**Claim tiers:** The pipeline parallelism architecture is **recommended** for multi-GPU PCIe setups based on bandwidth constraints and the vLLM TP+LoRA bug. Pipeline parallelism + QLoRA + vLLM LoRA compatibility is **expected** — it is the primary empirical question for Phase 0 environment validation. The GPU lease mechanism is **specified** in the architecture but **untested** at the implementation level.
   209→
   210→---
   211→
   212→[^hu2021lora]: Hu, E. J., et al. (2021). LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models. *arXiv:2106.09685*. [Full entry](references.md#hu2021lora)
   213→
   214→[^ha2016hypernetworks]: Ha, D., Dai, A., & Le, Q. V. (2016). HyperNetworks. *arXiv:1609.09106*. [Full entry](references.md#ha2016hypernetworks)
   215→
   216→[^charakorn2026doc2lora]: Charakorn, R., et al. (2026). Doc-to-LoRA: Learning to Instantly Internalize Contexts. *arXiv:2602.15902*. [Full entry](references.md#charakorn2026doc2lora)
   217→
   218→[^prabhakar2024lorasoups]: Prabhakar, A., et al. (2024). LoRA Soups: Merging LoRAs for Practical Skill Composition Tasks. *arXiv:2410.13025*. [Full entry](references.md#prabhakar2024lorasoups)
   219→
   220→[^cook2025pbb]: Cook, J., et al. (2025). Programming by Backprop: An Instruction is Worth 100 Examples When Finetuning LLMs. *arXiv:2506.18777*. [Full entry](references.md#cook2025pbb)
   221→
   222→[^zhang2025orthogonality]: Zhang, A., et al. (2025). Rethinking Inter-LoRA Orthogonality in Adapter Merging. *arXiv:2510.03262*. [Full entry](references.md#zhang2025orthogonality)
   223→
   224→[^zou2026merging]: Zou, J. (2026). Adapter Merging Reactivates Latent Reasoning Traces. *arXiv:2601.18350*. [Full entry](references.md#zou2026merging)
   225→
   226→[^dettmers2023qlora]: Dettmers, T., et al. (2023). QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs. *arXiv:2305.14314*. [Full entry](references.md#dettmers2023qlora)
   227→
   228→[^sheng2023slora]: Sheng, Y., et al. (2023). S-LoRA: Serving Thousands of Concurrent LoRA Adapters. *arXiv:2311.03285*. [Full entry](references.md#sheng2023slora)
   229→
```

     1→# Recursive Code Generation Loop
     2→
     3→## Overview
     4→
     5→The recursive loop is Rune's central execution mechanism: a task enters, code is generated, executed, evaluated, and — if tests fail — the trajectory feeds back into generation. When the loop terminates (tests pass or max attempts reached), the complete trajectory is distilled into a LoRA adapter. This document specifies the data flow at each step, retry logic, and termination conditions.
     6→
     7→For the serving architecture that supports this loop, see [GPU Strategy](multi-gpu-strategy.md). For where the resulting adapters are stored, see [Adapter Storage](adapter-storage.md).
     8→
     9→---
    10→
    11→## Loop Architecture
    12→
    13→```mermaid
    14→flowchart TD
    15→    Task([Task + Selected Adapters]) --> Generate
    16→    Generate[Generate Code] --> Execute
    17→    Execute[Execute in Sandbox] --> Reflect
    18→    Reflect{Tests Pass?} -->|no, attempts remaining| Generate
    19→    Reflect -->|yes| Distill
    20→    Reflect -->|no, max attempts reached| Distill
    21→    Distill[Distill Trajectory] --> Store
    22→    Store([Store Adapter in Registry])
    23→```
    24→
    25→---
    26→
    27→## Step-by-Step Data Flow
    28→
    29→### Step 1: Task Intake
    30→
    31→| Field | Description |
    32→|-------|-------------|
    33→| **Input** | Task description, test suite, selected adapter IDs from registry |
    34→| **Output** | Initialized agent state with loaded adapters |
    35→| **Side effects** | Adapter router queries registry, loads best-match adapters into lora-server |
    36→
    37→The adapter router queries the [adapter registry](adapter-storage.md) by task metadata (language, domain, task type) and selects the highest-fitness adapter at each hierarchy level. Selected adapters are loaded into the vLLM serving process via its dynamic LoRA API.
    38→
    39→### Step 2: Generate
    40→
    41→| Field | Description |
    42→|-------|-------------|
    43→| **Input** | Task description + trajectory so far (empty on first attempt) |
    44→| **Output** | Generated code (candidate solution) |
    45→| **Model** | Base SLM + loaded LoRA adapters, served via vLLM |
    46→
    47→On the first attempt, the prompt contains only the task description and test signatures. On subsequent attempts, the prompt includes the full trajectory: prior code attempts, execution outputs, error messages, and reflection notes. The trajectory grows monotonically — nothing is discarded between attempts.
    48→
    49→### Step 3: Execute
    50→
    51→| Field | Description |
    52→|-------|-------------|
    53→| **Input** | Generated code + test suite |
    54→| **Output** | Execution result: stdout, stderr, exit code, test results |
    55→| **Environment** | Docker sandbox: network-isolated, memory-limited, CPU-limited |
    56→
    57→The sandbox is a Docker container with no network access, read-only filesystem mounts (except the output directory), and strict resource limits. Agent-generated code cannot escape the container. The execution timeout is configurable per task but defaults to 30 seconds.
    58→
    59→### Step 4: Reflect
    60→
    61→| Field | Description |
    62→|-------|-------------|
    63→| **Input** | Execution result + current attempt count |
    64→| **Output** | Decision: retry, distill-on-success, or distill-on-exhaustion |
    65→| **Logic** | Tests pass -> distill. Tests fail AND attempts < max -> retry. Tests fail AND attempts >= max -> distill anyway. |
    66→
    67→The reflect step appends the execution result to the trajectory and evaluates termination conditions. The trajectory record includes the attempt number, generated code, full execution output, and the pass/fail determination. This record is the input to distillation regardless of outcome.
    68→
    69→### Step 5: Distill
    70→
    71→| Field | Description |
    72→|-------|-------------|
    73→| **Input** | Complete trajectory (all attempts, all execution results) |
    74→| **Output** | New LoRA adapter (`.safetensors` file) + metadata |
    75→| **Method** | Direct LoRA fine-tuning (Phase 3) or Doc-to-LoRA hypernetwork forward pass (Phase 4) |
    76→
    77→In Phase 3 (implementation plan), distillation uses direct LoRA fine-tuning: the trajectory is formatted as training data and a standard PEFT fine-tune produces the adapter. In Phase 4, the Doc-to-LoRA hypernetwork replaces this with a single forward pass — no gradient descent at inference time.
    78→
    79→Both successful and failed trajectories are distilled. A failed trajectory (max attempts reached, tests still failing) may still encode useful partial knowledge — debugging patterns, error recognition, partial solutions. The adapter metadata records whether the source trajectory was successful.
    80→
    81→### Step 6: Store
    82→
    83→| Field | Description |
    84→|-------|-------------|
    85→| **Input** | New adapter file + metadata (task type, pass rate, trajectory outcome) |
    86→| **Output** | Adapter registered in SQLite, `.safetensors` written to filesystem |
    87→| **Policy** | Write-once: no existing adapter is overwritten. See [Adapter Storage](adapter-storage.md). |
    88→
    89→---
    90→
    91→## Retry and Termination
    92→
    93→| Condition | Action | Trajectory State |
    94→|-----------|--------|-----------------|
    95→| Tests pass on attempt N | Terminate loop, distill | N attempts recorded, final attempt marked successful |
    96→| Tests fail, attempt < max | Retry from Generate | Trajectory grows by one attempt |
    97→| Tests fail, attempt = max | Terminate loop, distill | All attempts recorded, marked as exhausted |
    98→| Sandbox timeout | Treat as test failure | Timeout recorded in trajectory |
    99→| Sandbox crash (OOM, segfault) | Treat as test failure | Crash details recorded in trajectory |
   100→
   101→The maximum attempt count is configurable. The default is 5 attempts. This cap prevents indefinite loops on tasks beyond the model's current capability while still allowing multi-step reasoning.
   102→
   103→---
   104→
   105→## Trajectory Schema
   106→
   107→Each trajectory is a structured record passed to the distillation step:
   108→
   109→| Field | Type | Description |
   110→|-------|------|-------------|
   111→| `task_id` | string | Unique task identifier |
   112→| `task_description` | string | Natural language task description |
   113→| `test_suite` | string | Test code used for evaluation |
   114→| `attempts` | list | Ordered list of attempt records |
   115→| `attempts[].code` | string | Generated code for this attempt |
   116→| `attempts[].stdout` | string | Execution stdout |
   117→| `attempts[].stderr` | string | Execution stderr |
   118→| `attempts[].exit_code` | int | Process exit code |
   119→| `attempts[].tests_passed` | bool | Whether all tests passed |
   120→| `outcome` | enum | `success` or `exhausted` |
   121→| `adapter_ids_loaded` | list | Adapters that were active during generation |
   122→
   123→---
   124→
   125→## Integration Points
   126→
   127→- **Adapter Registry** ([Adapter Storage](adapter-storage.md)): Queried at task intake for adapter selection; written to at Store step
   128→- **vLLM lora-server** ([GPU Strategy](multi-gpu-strategy.md)): Serves base model + adapters during Generate step
   129→- **Sandbox**: Docker container managed by rune-agent, isolated from host
   130→- **model-training lib**: Provides PEFT utilities for the distillation step
   131→
```

```
     1→## Background
     2→
     3→### Memory Approaches for Language Model Agents
     4→
     5→Local coding agents — Aider, Cursor, Claude Code — operate within fixed context windows.
     6→As projects grow, agents lose access to earlier interactions: patterns discovered, bugs
     7→solved, architectural decisions made. Each new session begins without knowledge of prior
     8→sessions. The agent re-encounters the same failure modes, re-derives the same solutions,
     9→and cannot accumulate project-specific expertise over time.
    10→
    11→Memory approaches for LLM agents fall into three broad categories that differ in where
    12→and how knowledge is stored:
    13→
    14→**Token-space memory** (retrieval-augmented generation, long-context prompting, vector
    15→databases) retrieves text snippets and injects them into the context window before
    16→inference. Retrieval quality determines what the agent can recall, but every retrieved
    17→chunk consumes the same scarce resource — context tokens — that the agent needs for
    18→new instructions and code. Scaling memory capacity in this category means scaling
    19→context window size, which remains bounded and costly.
    20→
    21→**Destructive weight-space memory** (model editing via ROME or MEMIT, full fine-tuning)
    22→modifies base model weights directly. Each modification risks overwriting previously
    23→stored knowledge — a phenomenon known as catastrophic forgetting. Knowledge is entangled
    24→in shared parameters: storing new facts can corrupt existing capabilities because the
    25→same weights that encode one concept participate in encoding others.
    26→
    27→**Composable weight-space memory** (LoRA adapters, adapter libraries) stores knowledge
    28→as discrete, composable parameter deltas that augment the base model without modifying
    29→it. Each adapter can be loaded, removed, or replaced independently. The base model
    30→remains a stable reference point. Multiple adapters can coexist in a library, each
    31→encoding knowledge from a distinct session or task.
    32→
    33→Rune occupies the composable weight-space category. Each coding trajectory is distilled
    34→into a LoRA adapter that encodes the procedural knowledge from that session — patterns,
    35→fixes, architectural decisions — as parameter deltas. Adapters are write-once, immutable,
    36→and independently retrievable by task similarity.
    37→
    38→Pink et al. argue that episodic memory — single-shot learning of instance-specific
    39→contexts, retrievable and composable — is the missing capability for long-term LLM
    40→agents.[^pink2025episodic] Rune's adapter-per-session design maps directly onto this
    41→framework: each adapter is an episode, stored independently, and retrieved when a
    42→subsequent task resembles the original.
    43→
    44→---
    45→
    46→### LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation
    47→
    48→LoRA decomposes the weight update for a pre-trained weight matrix as a product of two
    49→low-rank matrices.[^hu2021lora] Rather than updating the full weight matrix \(W_0 \in
    50→\mathbb{R}^{d \times k}\) directly, LoRA reparameterizes the update as:
    51→
    52→\[
    53→\Delta W = BA, \quad B \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times r}, \; A \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times k}
    54→\]
    55→
    56→where rank \(r \ll \min(d, k)\). The trainable parameter count drops from \(dk\) to
    57→\(r(d + k)\), which is approximately \(2rd\) when \(d \approx k\). For a 7B-parameter
    58→model with typical hidden dimensions, rank 16–64 LoRA adapters require only a few
    59→million parameters — small enough to store hundreds of adapters on disk and load them
    60→on demand.
    61→
    62→During inference, the output becomes \(h = W_0 x + BAx\), where \(W_0\) is frozen.
    63→The adapter's contribution is additive and reversible: removing the adapter (setting
    64→\(\Delta W = 0\)) restores the original model behavior exactly. No modification to
    65→the base model is required.
    66→
    67→This reversibility is what makes LoRA viable as a memory substrate rather than merely
    68→a fine-tuning technique. Knowledge stored in one adapter does not interfere with
    69→knowledge stored in other adapters or in the base model — each adapter is a self-contained
    70→parameter delta that augments a stable foundation.
    71→
    72→---
    73→
    74→### QLoRA: Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation
    75→
    76→QLoRA extends LoRA with 4-bit NormalFloat (NF4) quantization of the frozen base model
    77→weights, combined with double quantization (quantizing the quantization constants
    78→themselves) and paged optimizers for memory spill management.[^dettmers2023qlora]
    79→
    80→The hardware relevance is significant. NF4 quantization reduces the memory footprint
    81→of a 7B-parameter base model from approximately 14 GB (bfloat16) to approximately 4 GB,
    82→enabling LoRA training and inference on consumer GPUs with limited VRAM. QLoRA makes it
    83→feasible to keep the base model loaded continuously while training or serving LoRA adapters
    84→within the VRAM budget of a typical consumer GPU.
    85→
    86→QLoRA is an efficiency layer in Rune's design, not a core algorithmic contribution.
    87→Rune's architecture does not depend on QLoRA specifically — any quantization scheme
    88→that fits the base model into available VRAM while preserving LoRA training quality
    89→would suffice. QLoRA is the practical default given current consumer hardware constraints.
    90→
    91→---
    92→
    93→### Multi-Adapter Serving: S-LoRA
    94→
    95→If each coding session produces an adapter, an agent accumulating hundreds of sessions
    96→needs a mechanism to serve the appropriate adapter at inference time without maintaining
    97→separate model copies for each one.
    98→
    99→S-LoRA introduces Unified Paging — a unified memory pool that manages adapter weights
   100→and KV cache tensors together in a shared memory space, enabling thousands of concurrent
   101→LoRA adapters to be served from a single base model instance.[^sheng2023slora] Adapters
   102→are loaded and swapped dynamically without reloading the base model. S-LoRA also
   103→introduces batched LoRA computation that amortizes the overhead of applying different
   104→adapters to different requests in the same batch.
   105→
   106→S-LoRA validates a core architectural assumption of Rune: that adapter-per-session
   107→memory is servable at scale from a single model instance. Rune's adapter library design
   108→— write-once adapters retrieved by task similarity — maps directly onto S-LoRA's
   109→concurrent serving model. The base model remains loaded; the appropriate adapter is
   110→swapped in per request based on retrieval results.
   111→
   112→---
   113→
   114→### Hypernetwork Architectures
   115→
   116→Hypernetworks — networks that generate the weights of another network — were introduced
   117→by Ha et al. as a relaxed form of weight-sharing.[^ha2016hypernetworks] Rather than
   118→learning fixed weights, a smaller meta-network learns to produce weights conditioned
   119→on some input signal. This decouples *what* is learned (the target network's behavior)
   120→from *how* it is parameterized (the meta-network's learned generation function).
   121→The paradigm enables instant adaptation: given a new conditioning input, the
   122→hypernetwork produces a new set of weights in a single forward pass.
   123→
   124→**Doc-to-LoRA** applies the hypernetwork paradigm to LoRA adapter generation.
   125→A hypernetwork takes a document as input and produces LoRA adapter weights in a single
   126→forward pass — no gradient-based fine-tuning required at inference time. The generated
   127→adapter encodes the document's content into the base model's parameter space, enabling
   128→needle-in-a-haystack question answering that outperforms RAG baselines on long
   129→documents.[^charakorn2026doc2lora]
   130→
   131→Doc-to-LoRA validates the hypernetwork-to-LoRA mechanism on textual documents and
   132→document QA tasks — retrieving specific facts from long textual inputs. It was not
   133→validated on coding trajectories, procedural knowledge, or code execution traces.
   134→Whether the same mechanism can encode the richer structure of code execution
   135→trajectories — sequential, causal, procedural — is the hypothesis Rune proposes to
   136→test. The transfer to a structurally different input type is the core open research
   137→question.
   138→
   139→**SHINE** is a concurrent hypernetwork system that maps in-context examples (few-shot
   140→demonstrations) to LoRA adapters in a single pass.[^liu2026shine] SHINE's input
   141→modality is demonstration examples — input-output pairs the LLM would otherwise process
   142→in-context. The hypernetwork converts this "in-context knowledge" to "in-parameter
   143→knowledge," eliminating the context cost of few-shot prompting at inference time.
   144→SHINE uses structured examples, not execution traces or documents — a distinction
   145→that becomes central to the trajectory modality argument in the final subsection.
   146→
   147→---
   148→
   149→### LoRA Composition Methods
   150→
   151→LoRA's additive structure (\(\Delta W = BA\)) suggests that multiple adapters could
   152→be combined — summed, concatenated, or interpolated — to merge skills from different
   153→training episodes into a single set of weights.
   154→
   155→Prabhakar et al. demonstrate that concatenation of LoRA adapters (the CAT method)
   156→outperforms data mixing for binary skill composition tasks.[^prabhakar2024lorasoups]
   157→This is the first empirical evidence that model merging can exceed training-data-level
   158→composition for skill acquisition — suggesting that adapters encode skills in
   159→structures that are, at least partially, combinable.
   160→
   161→However, composition is not straightforward. Zhang et al. show that orthogonality
   162→between merged LoRA modules does NOT guarantee semantic disentanglement.[^zhang2025orthogonality]
   163→Enforcing orthogonal adapter subspaces prevents direct parameter interference, but
   164→cannot ensure that composed adapters produce semantically coherent behavior. Two
   165→adapters can occupy orthogonal subspaces yet still produce conflicting behavioral
   166→outputs when applied together.
   167→
   168→Additional evidence from Zou demonstrates that merging adapters can cause unwanted
   169→re-emergence of latent reasoning traces due to partially misaligned update
   170→directions.[^zou2026merging] Composition can reactivate behaviors from individual
   171→adapters that were not intended to surface in the merged result.
   172→
   173→These interference results inform Rune's design choice: the default retrieval mode
   174→is single-adapter selection — the most relevant adapter loaded alone, not composed
   175→with others. Multi-adapter composition is an experimental extension. Rune's Evolution
   176→Operator (described in Methods) addresses composition through fitness-driven selection
   177→rather than naive merging, allowing the system to discover which adapter combinations
   178→produce beneficial behavior rather than assuming composition is safe by default.
   179→
   180→---
   181→
   182→### Programming by Backprop: Code as Procedural Abstraction
   183→
   184→Cook et al. demonstrate that declarative instructions — including code — can substitute
   185→for up to 100 execution examples when fine-tuning LLMs.[^cook2025pbb] A single
   186→well-structured instruction achieves comparable weight updates to training on dozens
   187→of input-output pairs. This result establishes that the format of training signal
   188→matters: procedural, instruction-like representations are more information-dense than
   189→equivalent example sets.
   190→
   191→This finding provides independent empirical support for Rune's core premise: that code
   192→execution trajectories — structured, procedural, and instruction-like — may be an
   193→efficient substrate for adapter training. If instructions can substitute for examples,
   194→then trajectories, which contain both the instructions (the code) and the procedural
   195→context (execution results, error messages, corrections), may encode knowledge more
   196→efficiently than input-output pair datasets of equivalent size.
   197→
   198→PBB also finds, however, that in-context instruction execution remains more reliable
   199→than weight-based procedural knowledge for the tasks they tested. This means the
   200→approach is promising but not guaranteed: there is a gap between what can be encoded
   201→in weights via backpropagation and what can be reliably executed from those weights.
   202→Rune must empirically validate whether trajectory-trained adapters close this gap for
   203→coding tasks specifically.
   204→
   205→---
   206→
   207→### Concurrent Hypernetwork Work and the Trajectory Modality Argument
   208→
   209→Multiple hypernetwork systems now exist for generating LoRA adapters from different
   210→input types. The key differentiator across these systems is the input modality — what
   211→goes in determines what kind of knowledge is encoded. Rune's proposed input modality,
   212→code execution trajectories, is structurally distinct from all existing systems.
   213→
   214→| System | Input Modality | Knowledge Encoded |
   215→|--------|---------------|-------------------|
   216→| Text-to-LoRA (Charakorn et al., 2025)[^charakorn2025t2l] | Natural language task descriptions | Declarative task specification — static, instructional |
   217→| Doc-to-LoRA (Charakorn et al., 2026) | Long documents | Factual content — informational, unstructured |
   218→| SHINE (Liu et al., 2026) | In-context examples (few-shot demos) | Demonstrated patterns — exemplary, structured pairs |
   219→| Rune (proposed) | Code execution trajectories | Procedural knowledge — sequential, causal, self-correcting |
   220→
   221→Code execution trajectories differ from the other three modalities in three structural
   222→ways:
   223→
   224→**Temporal ordering.** Steps in a trajectory have a meaningful causal sequence: code
   225→generation precedes execution, execution precedes error, error precedes diagnosis,
   226→diagnosis precedes fix. This temporal structure carries information about causality
   227→that is absent from documents (which present facts without an ordering obligation)
   228→and from examples (which present correct input-output pairs without the process that
   229→generated them).
   230→
   231→**Procedural abstraction.** A trajectory encodes a procedure — how to solve a problem
   232→step by step — rather than a fact (what is true), an instruction (what to do), or an
   233→example (what a correct pair looks like). Procedural knowledge is action-oriented:
   234→it specifies operations, their preconditions, and their effects.
   235→
   236→**Self-correcting structure.** Trajectories naturally contain failure-recovery patterns:
   237→attempt → error → diagnosis → fix. These patterns are absent from documents, instructions,
   238→and demonstrations. A trajectory that includes a bug and its correction encodes both
   239→the error pattern and its resolution in the same training signal.
   240→
   241→PBB's finding that instructions substitute for examples supports this argument from an
   242→independent direction: if procedural representations are efficient for weight-based
   243→learning, then trajectories — which are the richest procedural representation, containing
   244→both the procedure and its full execution context — may be the most efficient input
   245→modality for hypernetwork-based adapter generation. Whether this theoretical efficiency
   246→advantage holds in practice is a central empirical question for Rune. PBB validated
   247→instructions on a different task distribution; trajectories have not yet been tested
   248→as hypernetwork input.
   249→
   250→---
   251→
   252→[^pink2025episodic]: Pink, M., et al. (2025). Position: Episodic Memory is the Missing Piece for Long-Term LLM Agents. *arXiv:2502.06975*. [Full entry](references.md#pink2025episodic)
   253→
   254→[^hu2021lora]: Hu, E. J., et al. (2021). LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models. *arXiv:2106.09685*. [Full entry](references.md#hu2021lora)
   255→
   256→[^dettmers2023qlora]: Dettmers, T., et al. (2023). QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs. *arXiv:2305.14314*. [Full entry](references.md#dettmers2023qlora)
   257→
   258→[^sheng2023slora]: Sheng, Y., et al. (2023). S-LoRA: Serving Thousands of Concurrent LoRA Adapters. *arXiv:2311.03285*. [Full entry](references.md#sheng2023slora)
   259→
   260→[^ha2016hypernetworks]: Ha, D., Dai, A., & Le, Q. V. (2016). HyperNetworks. *arXiv:1609.09106*. [Full entry](references.md#ha2016hypernetworks)
   261→
   262→[^charakorn2026doc2lora]: Charakorn, R., et al. (2026). Doc-to-LoRA: Learning to Instantly Internalize Contexts. *arXiv:2602.15902*. [Full entry](references.md#charakorn2026doc2lora)
   263→
   264→[^liu2026shine]: Liu, Y., et al. (2026). SHINE: A Scalable In-Context Hypernetwork for Mapping Context to LoRA in a Single Pass. *arXiv:2602.06358*. [Full entry](references.md#liu2026shine)
   265→
   266→[^prabhakar2024lorasoups]: Prabhakar, A., et al. (2024). LoRA Soups: Merging LoRAs for Practical Skill Composition Tasks. *arXiv:2410.13025*. [Full entry](references.md#prabhakar2024lorasoups)
   267→
   268→[^zhang2025orthogonality]: Zhang, A., et al. (2025). Rethinking Inter-LoRA Orthogonality in Adapter Merging. *arXiv:2510.03262*. [Full entry](references.md#zhang2025orthogonality)
   269→
   270→[^zou2026merging]: Zou, J. (2026). Adapter Merging Reactivates Latent Reasoning Traces. *arXiv:2601.18350*. [Full entry](references.md#zou2026merging)
   271→
   272→[^cook2025pbb]: Cook, J., et al. (2025). Programming by Backprop: An Instruction is Worth 100 Examples When Finetuning LLMs. *arXiv:2506.18777*. [Full entry](references.md#cook2025pbb)
   273→
   274→[^charakorn2025t2l]: Charakorn, R., et al. (2025). Text-to-LoRA: Instant Transformer Adaption. *arXiv:2506.06105*. [Full entry](references.md#charakorn2025t2l)
   275→
```

```
     1→"""Trajectory recording and formatting for coding session distillation.
     2→
     3→Provides functions to persist, load, and convert coding session trajectories
     4→into SFT-compatible chat format for LoRA fine-tuning pipelines.
     5→"""
     6→
     7→from __future__ import annotations
     8→
     9→import json
    10→import os
    11→from datetime import datetime, timezone
    12→from pathlib import Path
    13→from typing import Any, Optional
    14→
    15→SYSTEM_PROMPT = "You are a Python code generator. Output only code, no explanation."
    16→
    17→
    18→def _get_trajectory_dir() -> Path:
    19→    """Return the trajectory storage directory, respecting RUNE_TRAJECTORY_DIR env var.
    20→
    21→    Reads env var inside function body (not module level) so that monkeypatch.setenv()
    22→    works correctly in tests.
    23→    """
    24→    env_dir = os.environ.get("RUNE_TRAJECTORY_DIR")
    25→    if env_dir:
    26→        return Path(env_dir)
    27→    return Path.home() / ".rune" / "trajectories"
    28→
    29→
    30→def record_trajectory(
    31→    session_id: str,
    32→    steps: list[dict[str, Any]],
    33→    outcome: Optional[str] = None,
    34→    *,
    35→    task_description: str = "",
    36→    task_type: str = "",
    37→    adapter_ids: list[str] | None = None,
    38→) -> dict[str, Any]:
    39→    """Persist a coding session trajectory to disk for future distillation.
    40→
    41→    Args:
    42→        session_id: Unique identifier for the coding session.
    43→        steps: List of step dicts, each containing attempt results.
    44→        outcome: Final session result ('success', 'exhausted', or None).
    45→        task_description: Natural language description of the coding task.
    46→        task_type: Category of task (e.g. 'function', 'class', 'refactor').
    47→        adapter_ids: LoRA adapter IDs used during the session.
    48→
    49→    Returns:
    50→        A dict with 'session_id' and 'file_path' keys.
    51→    """
    52→    trajectory_dir = _get_trajectory_dir()
    53→    trajectory_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    54→
    55→    file_path = trajectory_dir / f"{session_id}.json"
    56→    timestamp = datetime.now(tz=timezone.utc).isoformat()
    57→
    58→    trajectory: dict[str, Any] = {
    59→        "session_id": session_id,
    60→        "task_description": task_description,
    61→        "task_type": task_type,
    62→        "adapter_ids": adapter_ids if adapter_ids is not None else [],
    63→        "outcome": outcome,
    64→        "timestamp": timestamp,
    65→        "steps": steps,
    66→    }
    67→
    68→    file_path.write_text(json.dumps(trajectory, indent=2))
    69→
    70→    return {"session_id": session_id, "file_path": str(file_path)}
    71→
    72→
    73→def load_trajectory(trajectory_id: str) -> dict[str, Any]:
    74→    """Load a stored trajectory by session ID.
    75→
    76→    Args:
    77→        trajectory_id: The session ID used as the filename (without .json).
    78→
    79→    Returns:
    80→        A dict containing the full trajectory data including steps and metadata.
    81→
    82→    Raises:
    83→        FileNotFoundError: If no trajectory file exists for the given ID.
    84→    """
    85→    trajectory_dir = _get_trajectory_dir()
    86→    file_path = trajectory_dir / f"{trajectory_id}.json"
    87→    # Let FileNotFoundError propagate naturally if file does not exist
    88→    return json.loads(file_path.read_text())  # type: ignore[no-any-return]
    89→
    90→
    91→def format_for_sft(trajectory: dict[str, Any]) -> list[dict[str, str]]:
    92→    """Convert a trajectory into SFT-compatible chat format.
    93→
    94→    Only successful trajectories (outcome == 'success') produce output.
    95→    Extracts the final step where tests_passed is True as the assistant message.
    96→
    97→    Args:
    98→        trajectory: A trajectory dict as returned by load_trajectory.
    99→
   100→    Returns:
   101→        A list of 3 message dicts ([system, user, assistant]) for successful
   102→        trajectories, or an empty list if the trajectory did not succeed.
   103→    """
   104→    if trajectory.get("outcome") != "success":
   105→        return []
   106→
   107→    steps: list[dict[str, Any]] = trajectory.get("steps", [])
   108→    successful_step = next(
   109→        (s for s in reversed(steps) if s.get("tests_passed")),
   110→        None,
   111→    )
   112→
   113→    if successful_step is None:
   114→        return []
   115→
   116→    task_description: str = trajectory.get("task_description", "")
   117→    generated_code: str = successful_step.get("generated_code", "")
   118→
   119→    return [
   120→        {"role": "system", "content": SYSTEM_PROMPT},
   121→        {"role": "user", "content": task_description},
   122→        {"role": "assistant", "content": generated_code},
   123→    ]
   124→
```

```
     1→"""DocToLoraHypernetwork: Perceiver-based instant LoRA adapter generation.
     2→
     3→Generates rank-8 LoRA adapter weights from token IDs in a single forward pass.
     4→Distinct from the QLoRA gradient-descent path (Phase 21) — this produces adapters
     5→in <1s by cross-attending over token embeddings with learned latents.
     6→
     7→IMPORTANT: All GPU imports (torch, safetensors) are deferred inside function/method
     8→bodies per INFRA-05 pattern — this module is importable in CPU-only CI.
     9→
    10→Usage:
    11→    from model_training.hypernetwork import (
    12→        DocToLoraHypernetwork,
    13→        save_hypernetwork_adapter,
    14→    )
    15→
    16→    model = DocToLoraHypernetwork(input_dim=32000)
    17→    weights = model(token_ids)
    18→    save_hypernetwork_adapter(weights, "/tmp/adapter", "Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B")
    19→"""
    20→
    21→from __future__ import annotations
    22→
    23→import json
    24→from pathlib import Path
    25→from typing import TYPE_CHECKING, Any, Sequence
    26→
    27→if TYPE_CHECKING:
    28→    import torch
    29→
    30→
    31→def _build_hypernetwork_class() -> type:
    32→    """Build and return DocToLoraHypernetwork as a real nn.Module subclass.
    33→
    34→    This function is called the first time DocToLoraHypernetwork is instantiated
    35→    so that torch.nn.Module is only imported when needed (INFRA-05 pattern).
    36→    """
    37→    import torch  # noqa: PLC0415
    38→    import torch.nn as nn  # noqa: PLC0415
    39→
    40→    class _DocToLoraHypernetwork(nn.Module):
    41→        """Perceiver-based hypernetwork that generates LoRA adapter weights.
    42→
    43→        Given a sequence of token IDs (from a document/trajectory), produces a
    44→        PEFT-compatible state_dict with lora_A and lora_B matrices for every
    45→        target_module across all transformer layers — in a single forward pass.
    46→
    47→        Architecture:
    48→        - Lightweight learned token embedding (not the 7B base model's embedding)
    49→        - Learnable latent array (num_latents x latent_dim)
    50→        - Cross-attention: latents attend over token embeddings
    51→        - Self-attention stack: process latents
    52→        - Linear head: project to all LoRA weight parameters
    53→
    54→        Args:
    55→            input_dim: Vocabulary size for the hypernetwork's own token embedding.
    56→            num_latents: Number of learnable latent vectors. Default: 32.
    57→            latent_dim: Dimensionality of each latent vector. Default: 256.
    58→            depth: Number of self-attention layers over latents. Default: 4.
    59→            heads: Number of attention heads. Default: 8.
    60→            rank: LoRA rank for generated adapters. Default: 8.
    61→            target_modules: LoRA target module names. Default: ("q_proj", "v_proj").
    62→            num_layers: Number of transformer layers in target model. Default: 28.
    63→            hidden_dim: Hidden dim of q_proj/v_proj in target model. Default: 4096.
    64→        """
    65→
    66→        def __init__(
    67→            self,
    68→            input_dim: int,
    69→            num_latents: int = 32,
    70→            latent_dim: int = 256,
    71→            depth: int = 4,
    72→            heads: int = 8,
    73→            rank: int = 8,
    74→            target_modules: Sequence[str] = ("q_proj", "v_proj"),
    75→            num_layers: int = 28,
    76→            hidden_dim: int = 4096,
    77→        ) -> None:
    78→            super().__init__()
    79→
    80→            self.rank = rank
    81→            self.hidden_dim = hidden_dim
    82→            self.target_modules: list[str] = list(target_modules)
    83→            self.num_layers = num_layers
    84→            self.num_latents = num_latents
    85→            self.latent_dim = latent_dim
    86→
    87→            # Hypernetwork's own lightweight embedding — does NOT load the 7B model
    88→            self.token_embedding = nn.Embedding(input_dim, latent_dim)
    89→
    90→            # Learnable latent array: shape (num_latents, latent_dim)
    91→            self.latents = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(num_latents, latent_dim))
    92→
    93→            # Cross-attention: latents (Q) attend over token embeddings (K, V)
    94→            self.cross_attend = nn.MultiheadAttention(
    95→                latent_dim, heads, batch_first=True
    96→            )
    97→
    98→            # Self-attention stack to process latents
    99→            encoder_layer = nn.TransformerEncoderLayer(
   100→                latent_dim, heads, batch_first=True
   101→            )
   102→            self.self_attend = nn.TransformerEncoder(encoder_layer, num_layers=depth)
   103→
   104→            # Project flattened latents to all LoRA weight parameters.
   105→            # Each (layer, module) needs:
   106→            #   lora_A: rank * hidden_dim params
   107→            #   lora_B: hidden_dim * rank params
   108→            total_params = len(target_modules) * num_layers * 2 * rank * hidden_dim
   109→            self.weight_head = nn.Linear(latent_dim * num_latents, total_params)
   110→
   111→        def forward(self, token_ids: torch.Tensor) -> dict[str, torch.Tensor]:
   112→            """Generate PEFT-compatible LoRA adapter weights from token IDs.
   113→
   114→            Args:
   115→                token_ids: Integer token ID tensor of shape (batch, seq_len).
   116→
   117→            Returns:
   118→                Dict mapping PEFT state_dict keys to weight tensors.
   119→                Keys follow:
   120→                  base_model.model.model.layers.{i}.self_attn.{module}.lora_{A|B}.weight
   121→                Returns first batch element (one adapter per trajectory).
   122→            """
   123→            batch_size = token_ids.shape[0]
   124→
   125→            # Embed token IDs with hypernetwork's own lightweight embedding
   126→            x = self.token_embedding(token_ids)  # (batch, seq_len, latent_dim)
   127→
   128→            # Expand latents for batch dimension
   129→            latents = self.latents.unsqueeze(0).expand(
   130→                batch_size, -1, -1
   131→            )  # (batch, num_latents, latent_dim)
   132→
   133→            # Cross-attention: latents attend over token embeddings
   134→            latents, _ = self.cross_attend(
   135→                latents, x, x
   136→            )  # (batch, num_latents, latent_dim)
   137→
   138→            # Self-attention stack over latents
   139→            latents = self.self_attend(latents)  # (batch, num_latents, latent_dim)
   140→
   141→            # Flatten latents
   142→            latents = latents.reshape(
   143→                batch_size, -1
   144→            )  # (batch, num_latents * latent_dim)
   145→
   146→            # Project to all LoRA weight parameters
   147→            weights = self.weight_head(latents)  # (batch, total_weight_params)
   148→
   149→            # Reshape into PEFT state_dict for first batch element (index 0)
   150→            state_dict: dict[str, torch.Tensor] = {}
   151→            offset = 0
   152→            a_size = self.rank * self.hidden_dim
   153→            b_size = self.hidden_dim * self.rank
   154→
   155→            for i in range(self.num_layers):
   156→                for module in self.target_modules:
   157→                    # lora_A: shape (rank, hidden_dim)
   158→                    key_a = (
   159→                        f"base_model.model.model.layers.{i}"
   160→                        f".self_attn.{module}.lora_A.weight"
   161→                    )
   162→                    state_dict[key_a] = weights[0, offset : offset + a_size].reshape(
   163→                        self.rank, self.hidden_dim
   164→                    )
   165→                    offset += a_size
   166→
   167→                    # lora_B: shape (hidden_dim, rank)
   168→                    key_b = (
   169→                        f"base_model.model.model.layers.{i}"
   170→                        f".self_attn.{module}.lora_B.weight"
   171→                    )
   172→                    state_dict[key_b] = weights[0, offset : offset + b_size].reshape(
   173→                        self.hidden_dim, self.rank
   174→                    )
   175→                    offset += b_size
   176→
   177→            return state_dict
   178→
   179→    return _DocToLoraHypernetwork
   180→
   181→
   182→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   183→# DocToLoraHypernetwork: lazy proxy that builds the real nn.Module class on
   184→# first instantiation. This keeps the module importable without torch.
   185→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   186→
   187→
   188→class _LazyHypernetworkProxy:
   189→    """Lazy proxy for DocToLoraHypernetwork.
   190→
   191→    Acts as a callable that creates a real nn.Module instance on first call.
   192→    The class is rebuilt (and cached) on first instantiation so that torch is
   193→    only imported when actually needed (INFRA-05 pattern).
   194→    """
   195→
   196→    _real_class: type | None = None
   197→
   198→    def __call__(self, *args: Any, **kwargs: Any) -> Any:
   199→        if self._real_class is None:
   200→            self.__class__._real_class = _build_hypernetwork_class()
   201→        return self._real_class(*args, **kwargs)  # type: ignore[misc]
   202→
   203→    def __instancecheck__(self, instance: object) -> bool:
   204→        if self._real_class is None:
   205→            return False
   206→        return isinstance(instance, self._real_class)
   207→
   208→
   209→DocToLoraHypernetwork: _LazyHypernetworkProxy = _LazyHypernetworkProxy()
   210→
   211→
   212→def save_hypernetwork_adapter(
   213→    weights: dict[str, "torch.Tensor"],
   214→    output_dir: str,
   215→    base_model_id: str,
   216→    rank: int = 8,
   217→    target_modules: list[str] | None = None,
   218→) -> None:
   219→    """Serialize hypernetwork-generated LoRA weights in PEFT adapter format.
   220→
   221→    Writes:
   222→    - adapter_model.safetensors: the LoRA weight tensors
   223→    - adapter_config.json: PEFT-compatible configuration
   224→
   225→    Args:
   226→        weights: PEFT state_dict from DocToLoraHypernetwork.forward().
   227→        output_dir: Directory to write adapter files to (created if needed).
   228→        base_model_id: HuggingFace model ID of the base model.
   229→        rank: LoRA rank. Default: 8.
   230→        target_modules: List of module names. Default: ["q_proj", "v_proj"].
   231→
   232→    Note:
   233→        Does NOT include embed_tokens or lm_head — vLLM rejects these in adapters
   234→        (per Phase 21-01 decision: no modules_to_save in LoraConfig).
   235→    """
   236→    from safetensors.torch import save_file  # noqa: PLC0415
   237→
   238→    if target_modules is None:
   239→        target_modules = ["q_proj", "v_proj"]
   240→
   241→    output_path = Path(output_dir)
   242→    output_path.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
   243→
   244→    # Write adapter weights as safetensors
   245→    safetensors_path = output_path / "adapter_model.safetensors"
   246→    save_file(weights, str(safetensors_path))
   247→
   248→    # Write PEFT-compatible adapter_config.json
   249→    adapter_config: dict[str, object] = {
   250→        "peft_type": "LORA",
   251→        "r": rank,
   252→        "lora_alpha": rank * 2,
   253→        "target_modules": target_modules,
   254→        "lora_dropout": 0.0,
   255→        "bias": "none",
   256→        "task_type": "CAUSAL_LM",
   257→        "base_model_name_or_path": base_model_id,
   258→        "inference_mode": True,
   259→        "modules_to_save": None,
   260→        "fan_in_fan_out": False,
   261→    }
   262→
   263→    config_path = output_path / "adapter_config.json"
   264→    config_path.write_text(json.dumps(adapter_config, indent=2))
   265→
```
